\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)},

where
b=n+1-w^{-1}(i)
and
a=n+1-w^{-1}(i+1)
, and the notation
Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\lambda+\rho})^{s_{i}}\coloneqq Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\lambda+\rho})^{s_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})
used above refers to the usual permutation action on variables given in general for simple reflections
s_{i}
by

f^{s_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})=f^{s_{i}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\coloneqq f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},x_{i},x_{i+2},\cdots,x_{n}).

If
a<b
, then according to Figure
3.2
and a short computation, (
4.1
) above is precisely

Z(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda+\rho}_{s_{i}w})=T_{i}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}Z(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda+\rho}_{w}).

Moreover,
a<b
implies
w^{-1}(i)<w^{-1}(i+1)
, which is equivalent to
\ell(s_{i}w)>\ell(w)
, in which case
\mathcal{KN}_{s_{i}w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\lambda)=T_{i}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\lambda)
.
Thus, the partition functions
Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\lambda+\rho})
satisfy the same relation as the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\lambda)
under the operators
T_{i}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
and we conclude
Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\lambda+\rho})=\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\lambda)
for all
w\in S_{n}
and all integer partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
.
∎

By a similar argument, the specializations
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})
of the generalized key polynomials may be recognized as partition functions of the lattice model, up to a scalar which depends upon
\zeta
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\ldots,\zeta_{n})
be a weak composition, let
\zeta^{+}=(\zeta_{1}^{+},\zeta_{2}^{+},\ldots,\zeta_{n}^{+})
denote the unique partition obtained by permuting the parts of
\zeta
, and let
v_{\zeta}\in S_{n}
be the permutation of minimal length such that
v_{\zeta}\cdot\zeta=\zeta^{+}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{S}_{v_{\zeta}}^{\mu}
the system with
n
rows and
\zeta_{1}^{+}+1
columns whose boundary condition is determined by
v_{\zeta}
and the integer partition
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\mu_{n})
, where
\mu_{i}=\zeta_{1}^{+}-\zeta_{n+1-i}^{+}
.
Then

K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{C}\cdot Z(\mathcal{S}_{v_{\zeta}}^{\mu}),

where
C=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(-\alpha\beta\cdot h_{j-3}(\alpha,\beta)-\gamma\cdot h_{j-2}(\alpha,\beta))
in the notation of Proposition
2.2
.

Proof.

First consider the system
\mathcal{S}_{\text{id}}^{\mu}
with
n
rows and
\zeta_{1}^{+}+1
columns whose boundary condition is determined by the identity permutation
id
in
S_{n}
and the partition
\mu
.
By Proposition
2.2
,

Z(\mathcal{S}_{\text{id}}^{\mu})=C\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\zeta_{i}^{+}}=C\cdot K_{\zeta^{+}}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x}).

The solvability of the lattice model implies
Z(\mathcal{S}_{v_{\zeta}}^{\mu})=T_{v_{\zeta}}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}Z(\mathcal{S}_{\text{id}}^{\mu})=T_{v_{\zeta}}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}C\cdot\boldsymbol{x}^{\zeta^{+}}
, and since scalar multiplication commutes with the application of the operators
T_{i}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
, we conclude
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{C}\cdot Z(\mathcal{S}_{v_{\zeta}}^{\mu})
.
∎

When
\zeta
satisfies an additional hypothesis,
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
is a twisted Kirillov polynomial.

Corollary 4.2
.

If
\zeta
is a weak composition such that
\lambda\coloneqq\mu-\rho
is an integer partition, then
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})=\mathcal{KN}_{v_{\zeta}}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\lambda)
.

We note that
\lambda
is an integer partition if and only if
\zeta_{i}^{+}\geq\zeta_{i+1}^{+}+1
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

5.
Applications and Kirillov’s conjectures

As an application of our lattice model, we obtain several new results about the non-negativity of the coefficients of the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
for specializations of the parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
.
Kirillov’s conjecture that the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
have non-negative coefficients is already known to hold for numerous special cases.
Results for various choices of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and specializations of the variables
x_{i}
to
1
are discussed in
[
25
]
and the citations therein.
An even stronger partial result was proven by Chen and Zhang:

Theorem 5.1

(
[
19
, Theorem A]
)
.

Let
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
be parameters with
\gamma=0
and let
\eta=\alpha-\beta
.
Then for every integer
n\geq 2
and for every permutation
w\in S_{n}
,

\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})\in\mathbb{N}[\alpha,\beta,\eta][x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}].

Although Kirillov indicates the conjecture may be false for
\gamma=0
(see Comment (b) following Conjecture 4.9
[
25
]
), a formal counter-example has not previously appeared in the literature.
We thus present one here.

Proposition 5.2
.

There exist choices of
n>0
and
w\in S_{n}
for which the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
have negative coefficients.

Proof.

The smallest
n
for which this occurs is
n=4
, where exactly two
w\in S_{4}
give polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
with negative coefficients.
In cycle notation, they are
w=(1,3,2,4)
and
w=(1,4,2,3)
.
Their reduced words are
s_{3}s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}
and
s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}
, respectively, understood as acting on the left.
The resulting polynomials require several pages to record them, and so are excluded from this text.
Their negative terms in the monomial expansion are
-\beta^{3}\gamma x_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}-\beta^{2}\gamma x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}
and
-(\alpha+\gamma)\beta^{4}\gamma^{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}x_{4}^{2}-\beta^{4}\gamma^{2}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}x_{4}
, respectively.
∎

Similarly, the coefficients of
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})
are non-negative for many specializations of the parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma

[
25
, Lemma 4.18]
, but this does not hold in general, even with Kirillov’s restriction
\zeta\subset\rho
.

Proposition 5.3
.

There exist compositions
\zeta
for which the polynomials
K_{\zeta}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x})
have negative coefficients.

Proof.

Consider
\zeta=(1,2,2,1)\subset(4,3,2,1)
.
∎

As a corollary of these propositions, we resolve Kirillov’s
[
25
, Conjecture 4.9]
, as well as
[
25
, Conjecture 1.3]
, which is an analogous statement involving the parameter
d
.
Although Kirillov’s conjectures do not hold in general, we nevertheless identify the strongest case which does hold.
Our positive results for special cases of Kirillov’s conjectures are essentially a consequence of recovering the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
as partition functions of our lattice model by the following special case of Theorem
2.3
.

Corollary 5.4
.

Given a positive integer
n
, let
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\rho}
be a system whose grid has
n
rows and
n
columns, and whose boundary condition is determined by the integer partition
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)
and the permutation
w
in
S_{n}
.
Then
Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\rho})=\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
.

Having established an alternative method of computing the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
, we are in a position to prove that the coefficients of the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
are non-negative.
We first introduce the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
\gamma=0
, then in any admissible state of any lattice model with boundary conditions as defined in Section
2
, the vertical edges of every vertex have labels which contain at most one color.

Proof.

Suppose there exists an admissible state of the lattice model in which there is some vertex with a vertical edge whose label contains at least two colors.
Then a vertex of the form

a
+
\{a,b\}
\{b\}

necessarily occurs in the state also, since all paths must exit the grid on the left via separate rows.
The Boltzmann weight of this vertex is equal to
\beta\gamma
if
a<b
and
-\gamma
if
b<a
, so the Boltzmann weight of the state is divisible by
\gamma
and thus is equal to
0
in the specialization
\gamma=0
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\rho}
denote the system whose grid has
n
rows and
n
columns, and whose boundary condition is determined by
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)
and the permutation
w
in
S_{n}
, so that
Z(\mathcal{S}_{w}^{\rho})=\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
by Corollary
5.4
.
As shown in Figure
2.1
, if the Boltzmann weight of a vertex is necessarily expressed with a negative summand, then at least one of its adjacent vertical edges is labeled by a subset whose cardinality is greater than
1
.
By Lemma
5.5
, such a state cannot occur when
\gamma=0
.
Thus,
\gamma=0
implies that every admissible state has a non-negative Boltzmann weight and therefore
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})\in\mathbb{N}[\alpha,\beta][x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]
for all
w\in S_{n}
.
∎

Kirillov also presents the recursively defined
Di Francesco–Zinn-Justin polynomials

\mathcal{DZ}_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})

[
25
, Definition 4.4]
, in reference to
[
22
]
, which are obtained by the further specialization
\mathcal{DZ}_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})=\mathcal{KN}_{w^{-1}w_{0}}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
for
\alpha=\beta=1
and
\gamma=0

[
25
, Remark 4.7 (b)]
.
Kirillov’s conjecture that the Di Francesco–Zinn-Justin polynomials have non-negative coefficients
[
25
, Conjecture 4.5 (1)]
follows as an immediate special case of Theorem
1.4
:

Corollary 5.6
.

The Di Francesco–Zinn-Justin polynomials
\mathcal{DZ}_{w}(\boldsymbol{x})
have non-negative integer coefficients.

The above arguments use the fact that, although vertices with arbitrary subsets of colors assigned to their vertical edges may have non-zero Boltzmann weights even in the specialization
\gamma=0
, such vertices are prohibited from appearing in admissible states of the lattice model unless
\gamma\neq 0
.
In addition to this method of argument, our lattice model may more obviously be used to prove monomial positivity when all Boltzmann weights specialize to non-negative values.
Using this kind of argument, we recover the following result due to Liu
[
31
]
as another application of our lattice model.

Theorem 5.7

(
[
31
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

For each
w\in S_{n}
, the twisted Schubert polynomial

\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{w}=\mathcal{KN}_{w^{-1}w_{0}}^{(-1,-1,1)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})

has non-negative coefficients when expressed in the monomial basis.

Proof.

The Boltzmann weights in the specialization
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(-1,-1,1)
reduce to those in the figure below.

\hline\cr 1&1&1\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 5.1.
Boltzmann weights from Figure
2.1
in the specialization
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(-1,-1,1)
.
Note that
h_{k}(-1,-1)=(-1)^{k}(k+1)
.

There is one Boltzmann weight with a negative coefficient, but the corresponding vertex is prohibited from occurring in an admissible state of the lattice model by our choice of boundary conditions.
Thus, all Boltzmann weights are effectively non-negative, and so the coefficients of the partition functions
\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}_{w}
are as well.
∎

As noted in the Introduction, there is a corresponding increase in complexity of the Boltzmann weights when vertical edges are decorated with multiple colors, as it allows for phenomena such as the occurrence of negative signs and the complete homogeneous symmetric functions in
\alpha
and
\beta
of arbitrary degree.
Thus positivity results based on our lattice models are especially interesting in the case
\gamma\neq 0
.
We present another such example now.

Theorem 5.8
.

For all
n\geq 2
and any permutation
w
in
S_{n}
, the twisted Kirillov polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(-\alpha,-\beta,\alpha+\beta)}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
are in
\mathbb{N}[\alpha,\beta][x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

Proof.

By substitution, this is equivalent to the statement that the polynomials
\mathcal{KN}_{w}^{(\alpha,\beta,-(\alpha+\beta))}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0})
are in
\mathbb{N}[-\alpha,-\beta][x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}]
.
This follows from the fact that all our Boltzmann weights in the specialization
\gamma=-\alpha-\beta
are positive in
(-\alpha)
and
(-\beta)
and hence the partition functions, representing the twisted Kirillov polynomials, are positive as well.

The two most difficult sets of manipulations to see positivity in
(-\alpha)
and
(-\beta)
are handled as follows.
Using the specialization
\gamma=-\alpha-\beta
together with the identity
h_{k}=\alpha h_{k-1}+\beta^{k}
, we may rewrite the Boltzmann weights

\displaystyle(\dagger)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\Sigma|}(\beta^{|\Sigma|}-(\beta+\gamma)\cdot h_{|\Sigma|-1}(\alpha,\beta)\cdot(x\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}1))

\displaystyle(\ddagger)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\Sigma|-1}(-\beta)^{|\Sigma_{[c+1,n]}|}(\beta^{|\Sigma|-1}-(\beta+\gamma)\cdot h_{|\Sigma|-2}(\alpha,\beta))

as

\displaystyle(\dagger)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\Sigma|}(\beta^{|\Sigma|}+\alpha\cdot h_{|\Sigma|-1}(\alpha,\beta)\cdot(1-\beta x))=(-1)^{|\Sigma|}(h_{|\Sigma|}(\alpha,\beta)-\alpha\beta h_{|\Sigma|-1}(\alpha,\beta)x)

\displaystyle(\ddagger)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\Sigma|-1}(-\beta)^{|\Sigma_{[c+1,n]}|}(\beta^{|\Sigma|-1}+\alpha\cdot h_{|\Sigma|-2}(\alpha,\beta))=(-1)^{|\Sigma|-1}(-\beta)^{|\Sigma_{[c+1,n]}|}h_{|\Sigma|-1}(\alpha,\beta).

Applying the identity
h_{k}(\alpha,\beta)=(-1)^{k}h_{k}(-\alpha,-\beta)
arising from the homogeneity, we see that both Boltzmann weights
(\dagger)
and
(\ddagger)
are positive in
(-\alpha)
and
(-\beta)
:

\displaystyle(\dagger)

\displaystyle=h_{|\Sigma|}(-\alpha,-\beta)+(-\alpha)(-\beta)h_{|\Sigma|-1}(-\alpha,-\beta)x

\displaystyle(\ddagger)

\displaystyle=(-\beta)^{|\Sigma_{[c+1,n]}|}h_{|\Sigma|-1}(-\alpha,-\beta).

The remaining Boltzmann weights are easily seen to be positive in
(-\alpha)
and
(-\beta)
, remembering that
x\oplus_{\alpha,\gamma}1=1-\beta x
and
x\oplus_{\beta,\gamma}1=1-\alpha x
.
∎

The above positivity proof is, from the lattice model point of view, much more fascinating than Kirillov’s positivity conjectures proved in Theorem
1.4
(the case
\gamma=0
).
Indeed when the parameter
\gamma=\alpha+\beta
is non-zero, we witness all of the additional complexity in the lattice states and their Boltzmann weights (given in Figure
2.1
) described above.
It would be very interesting to provide a geometric connection to explain the positivity in either of Theorems
1.4
or
5.8
, and to try to develop the most general positivity results from this three-parameter family of twisted Kirillov polynomials.

Appendix A
Alternative Boltzmann weights

As a final remark, we record an alternative solvable lattice model for computing the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials.
It was constructed during the Polymath Jr Program in 2023 as a preliminary tool for proving Kirillov’s Conjecture
1.3
for the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials, prior to defining the lattice model presented in the main text.
It has the advantage of providing a simpler proof of Theorem
1.4
.
This lattice model is composed of an
n\times n
grid, and in the same manner as described in Section
2.2
, it is assigned a boundary condition determined by a permutation
w
in
S_{n}
and the integer partition
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)
.
Like the lattice model given in Section
2
, its admissible states consist of
n
paths which travel downward and leftward from the top boundary edges to the left boundary edges.
By an argument nearly identical to the one given in Section
4
, its partition functions are precisely the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
.
Denote by
S_{w}^{\rho}
the system whose grid has
n
rows and
n
columns, whose boundary condition is determined by the permutation
w
in
S_{n}
and the integer partition
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)
, and whose vertices take Boltzmann weights as dictated by Figure
A.1
.
Then

Z(S_{w}^{\rho})=\mathcal{KN}^{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0)}_{w}(\boldsymbol{x};\boldsymbol{0}).

Since all Boltzmann weights in Figure
A.1
are non-negative, this lattice model yields a manifestly positive formula for the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials, thereby providing a direct proof of Theorem
1.4
.

\hline\cr 1\hfil\hskip 15.0&x_{i}\hfil\hskip 15.0&\alpha\beta x_{i}\hfil\hskip 15.0&1\hfil\hskip 15.0&(1+\alpha x_{i})(1+\beta x_{i})\hfil\hskip 15.0\\

Figure A.1.
Boltzmann weights which yield partition functions that are the Hecke–Grothendieck polynomials.
Here
a<b
and
c
is arbitrary.
Note that these Boltzmann weights are not obtained from Figure
2.1
by setting
\gamma=0
.

The
R
-vertices which give a solution to the Yang–Baxter equation for the lattice model are pictured in Figure
A.2
below.
Their Boltzmann weights were calculated using a modification of code authored by H. Gustafsson.

\hline\cr(1+\beta x_{j})(1+\alpha x_{i})\hfil\hskip 10.&(1+\beta x_{i})(1+\alpha x_{j})\hfil\hskip 10.&(1+\beta x_{i})(1+\alpha x_{i})\hfil\hskip 10.&(1+\beta x_{j})(1+\alpha x_{j})\hfil\hskip 10.\\

Figure A.2.
Solution to the Yang–Baxter equation given by the Boltzmann weights in Figure
A.1
.
Note that these Boltzmann weights are not obtained from those in Figure
3.2
by setting
\gamma=0
.

This lattice model fundamentally differs from the one presented in Section
2
.
Most notably, multiple colors are prohibited from occupying the same vertical edge of a vertex.
Note that the Boltzmann weights in Figure
A.1
are not specializations of those in Figure
2.1
upon setting
\gamma=0
, even when the number of colors on a vertical edge is restricted to
0
or
1
:
a vertex of type
\tt{a}_{1}
has Boltzmann weight
1
, whereas the corresponding vertex in Figure
2.1
has Boltzmann weight
1+(\alpha+\beta)x_{i}
when
\gamma=0
.
Similarly, the Boltzmann weights in Figure
A.2
are not specializations of those in Figure
3.2
, as the Boltzmann weight of type
\tt{A}
is not a simultaneous specialization of types
\tt{A}_{1}
and
\tt{A}_{2}
.
Algebraically, this is a consequence of a differing connection to quantum group modules, as in the comment preceding Theorem
3.5
.

We propose that the
R
-matrix and
T
-matrix appearing in
[
14
]
are Drinfeld twists of our
R
-matrix and
L
-matrix, respectively, after a specialization of parameters and a change of basis.
We prove this for the case
n=1
.
The parameters of each lattice model are first specialized so that their partition functions are the
\beta
-Grothendieck polynomials: set

\alpha=0
for the Boltzmann weights in Figures
A.1
and
A.2
, and set
q=0
and
y_{i}=0
for all
i
for the Boltzmann weights in
[
14
, Figure 3, Figure 5]
.
Then make the change of basis in
[
14
]
so that the label
+
is regarded as less than than any color, to be consistent with the conventions stated in the proof of Theorem
3.4
.
Let

F=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&\beta&\\
&1&1&\\
&&&1\end{bmatrix}.

From Figure
A.1
in the specialization
\alpha=0
, for
n=1
we have

L=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&x_{i}&1+\beta x_{i}&\\
&1&0&\\
&&&1\\
\end{bmatrix}.

From
[
14
, Figure 3]
in the specialization
q=0
,
y_{i}=0
, and the reordered basis, we have

T=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&x_{i}&1&\\
&1+\beta x_{i}&0&\\
&&&1\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Then

\displaystyle F_{21}LF^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&1&1&\\
&\beta&1&\\
&&&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&x_{i}&1+\beta x_{i}&\\
&1&0&\\
&&&1\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&&&\\
&\frac{-1}{\beta-1}&\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}&\\
&\frac{1}{\beta-1}&-\frac{1}{\beta-1}&\\
&&&1\end{bmatrix}=T.

A similar computation shows that for
n=1
, the
R
in
[
14
]
with the same specialization and change of basis is a Drinfeld twist of our
R
by the same
F
.

#----------------------Script

for

verifying

the

Yang--Baxter

equation---------------------

#

The

following

script

generates

all

boundary

conditions

it

suffices

to

consider,

computes

#

the

partition

functions

of

the

two

resulting

lattice

models

for

each

boundary

condition,

#

and

tests

them

for

equality.

#

#

It

proceeds

by

first

generating

lists

[a,

b,

c,

d]

of

horizontal

boundary

edge

labels

#

for

all

choices

of

a,

b,

c,

d

zero

or

non-zero

and

all

orderings

of

the

non-zero

labels.

#

#

For

each

resulting

list

[a,

b,

c,

d]

it

next

computes

all

pairs

of

northern

boundary

#

edge

labels

Sigma

and

southern

boundary

edge

labels

Sigmaprime

for

which

there

could

#

exist

an

admissible

state

of

the

lattice

model.

#

Generate

binary

lists

for

horizontal

labels

0

or

non-zero

horiz_on_off

=

[]

for

i
,

j
,

k
,

l

in

itertools
.
product
({0,1},
